View up\-to\-date course information for 2026 Summer Term 1 and Term 2 on Workday Student, UBC’s new student information system, by logging into MyWorkday\.

### Multimedia Journalism

### JRNL 320

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

2024 Winter Term 2 Credits: 3 Pre\-requisite

Principles of multimedia storytelling with hands\-on journalistic methods in information gathering, fact\-checking, and ethical decision making\. *Prerequisite:* One of JRNL 200, JRNL 201\.

Sections \(1\)

### Feminist Postcolonial Critique and Journalism in a Digital Age

### JRNL 400

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

callison\-candis young\-mary\-lynn

2020 Winter Term 1 Credits: 3

This course will look at feminist and postcolonial critiques related to professional norms and practices of journalists, examining underlying democratic ideals in a rapidly evolving media landscape where news organizations are struggling to find their place amidst newly emerging digital infrastructures that are being coproduced with societal relations\.

### Special Topics in Contemporary Journalism \- REPRT INDGENOUS

### JRNL 520D

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

bellrichard\-chantelle gretsinger\-kathryn

2020 Winter Term 2 Credits: 3

This course offers students a unique opportunity to study and practice reporting in Indigenous communities in the Lower Mainland\. Students will learn about local First Nations cultures and history; examine representations of Indigenous peoples in Canadian media; and discuss strategies for in\-depth coverage of Indigenous issues\.

### Special Topics in Contemporary Journalism \- INTERVIEWING

### JRNL 520I

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

gretsinger\-kathryn

2020 Winter Term 1 Credits: 1\.5

This six\-week module is designed to allow students to immerse themselves in listening and thinking about ideas and audio storytelling\. The course focuses on core audio storytelling skills like focus, story development, interviewing and sound gathering\. It addresses the technical aspects of audio storytelling and the factors that turn good stories into great ones\.

    **Journalism Research in Practice**
    
    This course is mandatory for students planning doing a Thesis or Final Research Project in their second year\. This course will begin at the start of the term and run for six weeks, with classes every other week\.

### Media Ethics and Leadership

### JRNL 533

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

barforoush\-saranaz lapointe\-kirk

2020 Winter Term 1 Credits: 3

This course will look at the role imagined for journalists and media organizations in a democracy, and consequent professional norms, obligations, dilemmas, and expectations\. Using varied texts and case studies, this course will discuss, study, and debate journalism ethics and leadership in changing media landscapes\.

### Media Law

### JRNL 534

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

burnett\-daniel

2020 Winter Term 2 Credits: 3

Through this course students will be introduced to the Canadian legal system, court procedures, terminology, decisions and statutes\. Legal issues and pitfalls faced by journalists, including defamation, contempt, publication bans, privacy, intellectual property, freedom of expression, roadblocks to reporting, dealing with police situations and the law affecting online media, blogs, digital forum issues and citizen…

### JRNL 550

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

beers\-david mackinnon\-james

When the power of voice and narrative storytelling are joined with strong reporting and the right structure, journalism connects with the reader\. This seminar\-style class allows students to develop written projects intended to make an impact\.

### Writing and Research in the Disciplines

### WRDS 150

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

richards\-kimberly el\-bezre\-nazih beales\-meredith kim\-mi\-young knijnik\-baumvol\-laura lee\-tara saunders\-mary\-ann fitzpatrick\-katie schandorf\-michael

2026 Summer Credits: 3

Writing and reading in disciplines across the academy, focusing on practices that research disciplines share and those that differentiate them\. Restricted to students not in the Faculty of Arts\. Credit can be granted for only one of WRDS\_V 150 or WRDS\_V 151\. Equivalency: WRDS\_V 151\.

### Writing and Research in the Disciplines

### WRDS 150

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

foung\-dennis lee\-tara andrews\-thomas otto\-jonathan kim\-mi\-young lou\-adrian el\-bezre\-nazih thieme\-katja schandorf\-michael rea\-jaclyn blake\-susan ferreira\-laila knijnik\-baumvol\-laura hasanova\-dilia wu\-sang carruthers\-den\-hoed\-rebecca\-suzanne gagnon\-jennifer fitzpatrick\-katie

2026 Winter Term 1/2 Credits: 3

Writing and reading in disciplines across the academy, focusing on practices that research disciplines share and those that differentiate them\. Restricted to students not in the Faculty of Arts\. Credit can be granted for only one of WRDS\_V 150 or WRDS\_V 151\. Equivalency: WRDS\_V 151\.

### Writing and Research in the Disciplines \- RSRCH&amp;WRIT HUMAN

### WRDS 150A

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

fitzpatrick\-kathleen el\-bezre\-nazih carrabre\-m\-gillian lee\-tara burgess\-diane mania\-kirby bittner\-thomas\-jacob cowe\-jennifer andrews\-thomas cree\-dylan standing\-jade gagnon\-jennifer saunders\-mary\-ann schandorf\-michael fleet\-darren byrne\-connor sigurdson\-krista wu\-sang\-yin green\-william karpe\-rohan connolly\-andrew rea\-jaclyn beales\-meredith otto\-jonathan

2020 Winter Term 1/2 Credits: 3

Writing and reading in disciplines across the academy, focusing on practices that research disciplines share and those that differentiate them\.

    #### Public Notes
    
    All sections of WRDS 151 are **reserved** for students in the Faculty of Arts\.
    
    All WRDS sections are conducted synchronously\.     #### Public Notes
    
    This course invites students to explore key approaches to writing and communication by
    
    critically examining an array of texts—from podcasts to policy briefs, from memes to news
    
    articles, from Reddit posts to research articles, from Tik Tok videos to testimonials—and how
    
    these are used in context to produce knowledge, build worlds, construct identities, and
    
    exercise power\. The course introduces students to writing and communication as a field of
    
    study, including key principles that underpin the field, enduring and pressing issues that are transforming the field, and methods of analysis widely used in the field to make sense of how writing and communication are produced, circulated, and used in different cultural, academic, and professional contexts\. Students will analyze real\-world case studies of writing and communication, focusing on how audiences, contexts, and the purposes of a variety of texts shape meaning, ideology, and power\. 
    
    This is a required course for the Minor in Writing and Communication: [Minor in Writing and Communication \- School of Journalism, Writing, and Media \(ubc\.ca\)](https://jwam.ubc.ca/undergraduate/writing-studies-programs/minor-in-writing-and-communication/)
    
      
    
    **Registration priority** from the waitlist to the course will be given to students who follow the procedures below:
    
    -   **Make available** \- space on one's course timetable to avoid conflict with other courses\.
    -   **Have enough credits \-** to take the course\.
    -   **Have no active** registration holds \- \(i\.e\. finance\)
    
      
    
    *Students are welcome to swap themselves into another WRDS 200 section if seats become available\.*

### Multimodal Writing

### WRDS 340

*keyboard\_arrow\_down*

dadugblor\-stephen

2025 Winter Term 1 Credits: 3 Pre\-requisite

Creation of multimodal texts, including audio, visual, and alphabetical modes, for different audiences, purposes, and contexts\. Examines the impact of digital technologies on writers' considerations of purpose, audience, design, accessibility and ethics\. Restricted to students with at least third year standing\. Prerequisite: 3 credits from one of WRDS\_V 150, WRDS\_V 151, WRDS\_V 350, ENGL\_V 100, SCIE\_V 113, SCIE\_V 300, CHEM\_V 300, APSC\_V 176, LFS\_V 150, FRST\_V 150, NRES\_V 150, COMM\_V 196, Arts One, ASTU\_V 100, ASTU\_V 101, CAP\_V 100\.

    #### Public Notes
    
    This course introduces students to the possibilities and constraints of writing in multiple modes\. Working from the idea that the 21st century presents opportunities to engage a range of communities using digital technologies, the course offers students a space to discuss key theoretical issues associated with multimodal writing, including accessibility \(like closed captioning and subtitles\) and ethical considerations\. Students will learn about the how writing sonically, for example, connects to deafness, silence, and noise\. Through a variety of hands\-on projects, students will write in multiple modes, including audio, visual, and multimodal forms for different kinds of audiences, purposes, and contexts\. By so doing, the course enhances students’ knowledge in writing for professional, personal, and civic purposes\.
    
    Additional information can be found here: [WRDS Undergraduate Course Descriptions \- School of Journalism, Writing, and Media \(ubc\.ca\)](https://jwam.ubc.ca/wrds-undergraduate-courses/?filter=&amp;amp;search=) \.
    
    This is an elective course for the Minor in Writing and Communication: [Minor in Writing and Communication \- School of Journalism, Writing, and Media \(ubc\.ca\)](https://jwam.ubc.ca/undergraduate/writing-studies-programs/minor-in-writing-and-communication/)

    #### Public Notes
    
    This course introduces students to the possibilities and constraints of writing in multiple modes\. Working from the idea that the 21st century presents opportunities to engage a range of communities using digital technologies, the course offers students a space to discuss key theoretical issues in multimodal writing, including accessibility \(like closed captioning and subtitles\), Generative AI, and ethical considerations\. Among others, students will learn about how sonic writing connects to deafness, silence, and noise, and how visuality is shaped by power and politics\. Through a variety of hands\-on projects, students will write in multiple modes, including audio, visual, and multimodal forms for different kinds of audiences, purposes, and contexts\. By so doing, the course enhances students’ knowledge in writing for professional, personal, and civic purposes\.

    #### Public Notes
    
    # "A UBC study found that…"
    
    Did that catch your eye for a moment? That’s no accident\. It’s one of the many rhetorical strategies new media writers use to turn complex research into content that resonates with the public\. In this course, you’ll explore how research writing is transformed for wider audiences through social media, podcasts, blogs, and other new media platforms\. By the end, you’ll be ready to communicate research as a Digital Content Creator, Social Media Strategist, Knowledge Mobilization Specialist, Podcast Producer — and in many other roles at the intersection of research, storytelling, and media\.
    
    Additional information can be found here: [WRDS Undergraduate Course Descriptions \- School of Journalism, Writing, and Media \(ubc\.ca\)](https://jwam.ubc.ca/wrds-undergraduate-courses/?filter=&amp;amp;search=) \.
    
    This is an elective course for the Minor in Writing and Communication: [Minor in Writing and Communication \- School of Journalism, Writing, and Media \(ubc\.ca\)](https://jwam.ubc.ca/undergraduate/writing-studies-programs/minor-in-writing-and-communication/)

    #### Public Notes
    
    ## Are you passionate about community engagement and want to learn what reciprocal knowledge exchange between university and community looks like?
    
    This course will examine models of communication, engagement, and knowledge sharing between university researchers and other communities, and apply scholarly research to community\-engaged work that incorporates ethical communication and reflection\.
    
    Additional information can be found here: [WRDS Undergraduate Course Descriptions \- School of Journalism, Writing, and Media \(ubc\.ca\)](https://jwam.ubc.ca/wrds-undergraduate-courses/?filter=&amp;amp;search=) \.
    
    This is an elective course for the Minor in Writing and Communication: [Minor in Writing and Communication \- School of Journalism, Writing, and Media \(ubc\.ca\)](https://jwam.ubc.ca/undergraduate/writing-studies-programs/minor-in-writing-and-communication/)

    #### Public Notes
    
    ## Interested in science communication, environmental communication, community\-engaged scholarship or knowledge translation, mobilization, or exchange?  
    
      
    
    **WRDS 380** is a hands\-on introduction to communicating research with and for communities outside the university\. Students will work with a community partner to learn what research they need communicated, craft what they need, and receive constructive feedback from community members\. Students will also reflect on knowledge the community partner has shared with them and the benefits and challenges of knowledge exchange between universities and communities\. Balance  practical skills with ethical, evidence\-based practice\. Our community partner in Winter Term 2 will be CPCIL **\[**[**https://cpcil\.ca/**](https://cpcil.ca/)**\]**\.

    #### Public Notes
    
    How do Disabled folks use writing to challenge ableist practices of knowledge making? This course centres the intersectional epistemologies that Disabled writers create through their existence and articulation of their experiences in a world not built to include Disabled modes of life and expression\. Through Disability Justice and Crip Theory, students will analyze how Disabled writers disrupt, dismantle, and reimagine structures that dictate whose bodies/minds in the institution are considered valid\. By examining how Disabled writers “Crip” authoritative, normative standards of writing to create a more accessible relationship between writing and knowledge, students will develop their own cripped research practices that contribute to the transformation of scholarly ways of knowing, writing, and communicating\. This course is designed using inclusive and universal design principles that centre the access needs of everyone\.

    #### Public Notes
    
    In writing studies, “translingualism” emerged as a concept in the last decade or so alongside prior related concepts \(such as code\-meshing, multilingualism, etc\.\)\. Like its predecessors, it provides an alternative to the monolingualism that forces marginalized—often racialized and/or \(im\)migrant—language learners into assimilating into standardized, colonial discourses\. A consequence of cultural mixing, translingualism might generatively align with decolonial cultural theories that suggest dynamic, liberatory expressive performance capable of defying dominant logics of capture\. This special topics course unpacks current debates in/across writing and cultural studies around the communicative politics of translingual composition\(s\)\. It particularly embraces autoethnographic methodologies as a means of performing translingual writing studies grounded in researcher reflexivity and reflectivity\. As a story\-based \(or “counterstorying”\) methodology, autoethnography aligns with the cultural politics of translingualist critique of Western communicative subjectivity\.
